ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u1	Dear fellow Members and fellow Members, ladies and gentlemen! Starting the 25th meeting of the National Assembly. I am informed that today's meeting cannot be held by the following Member and Members: Alenka Jeraj, Mag. Stefan Tisel, Joško Godec, Dr. Pavel Gantar, Vasja Klavora from 11 a.m. to 11 p.m., Dr. Vinko Gorenak, Milenko Ziherl until 4 p.m., Renata Brunskole until 3 p.m. I welcome everyone here. We're moving to 28. On the day-to-day basis, this is: the debate on the reply of the Prime Minister to the parliamentary question of a new year in connection with the planned priorities of the government until the end of the mandate. At its 24th meeting, the National Assembly decided to hold a discussion on the response submitted by Prime Minister Borut Pahor to the question of Mr Joško Godc concerning the government's planned priorities by the end of the term of office. I give my word to the President of the Government for further explanation. Here you go, please.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u2	Thank you very much, Mr Vice President. Ladies and gentlemen! Good day and a good day to all of us. The Slovenian Government has decided to further answer the question that has been raised with particular pleasure because it would like to eliminate in the public part the impression that it does not have a vision and a plan for concrete steps to achieve this vision. By the end of this mandate, the government will have the following priorities: consolidating public finances, improving the conditions for business operations, improving payment discipline, improving the functioning of the labour market, more efficient prosecution of economic and organised crime and structural measures and institutional adjustments. Let's go in one order. These six priorities will require the adoption of some 27 laws – and I would like to thank this high house for not being too doubtful in public that they would not be able to get it under the roof. When we adopted the 27 legislative measures framework of the exit strategy at the beginning of 2010, we were dealing with an old Slovenian policy disease, which is to adopt conclusions and not implement them. We have overcome this disease, four-fifths of the laws in the exit strategy are now, even thanks to your very dedicated work, under the roof, valid and have an appropriate effect. Let's look at the first priority, the consolidation of public finances. Its aim is to gradually reduce the government deficit below 3% of GDP, stabilise public debt without additional tax burdens, with greater tax collection efficiency. The objective is to finance the development and necessary infrastructure and to continue to make more efficient use of European funds, and all of this, of course, means a more efficient use of state money for our needs. The second priority step is to improve the conditions for business operations. The objective of this priority is to enable companies to be restructured more quickly through the stimulating taxation of the balance sheet sum of banks, to increase the credit potential available to good companies, to provide businesses with the most transparent and easy access to the various financial instruments offered by the State. The third priority is to improve payment discipline. It is a matter of establishing mandatory reasonable payment periods, limiting the possibility of applying a deductible value-added tax only for accounts settled within the statutory period, introducing a currency as an instrument for securing payments to subcontractors and excluding from public tenders all operators that do not have a settlement of their obligations to subcontractors – a problem that is acute and we are solving at this moment. The fourth priority is to improve the functioning of the labour market. We want to change the working relationship towards simpler, faster and more efficient procedures for concluding and terminating the working relationship and facilitating transitions between jobs and employers. It is therefore not only that the Government wants to introduce procedures for faster forgiveness, as is commonly said, but also for faster employment, so that we can hunt conjunctural cycles on the labour market. We want to reduce risks, increase safety in the payment of maternity leave for workers and employers. The fifth priority is more effective prosecution of economic crime, widespread seizure and confiscation of property through reverse evidence, the acceleration of criminal proceedings through the introduction of negotiations between the prosecution and defence, the identification of public financial crimes. Finally, structural measures and institutional adjustments. Moreover, Mr Vice-President, because time does not allow me to say that the Government has also adopted a document setting out measures to increase the competitiveness of the Slovenian economy. We will do everything to adapt our actions to the expectations covered by the Stability Pact proposed by Germany and France for a faster recovery in the euro area. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u3	Thank you very much. The following is a presentation of the views of the groups of Members. Mr Franci Kek has the floor on behalf of the Zares Group.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u4	Thank you for your word. Honourable President of the Government, ministers and ministers, colleagues and colleagues! In the Group of Members, we really do not think that there are many reasons for optimism. Slovenia is in a crucial year when we are facing a fiscal crisis in the country. The delay of about one year has proved to be the true depth of the economic crisis, we are facing EU requirements or proposals from the so-called German-French import of reforms in the euro area, and the policy is in the midst of referendum demands and threats, and the field of dialogue with social partners is getting stronger. We must not keep our eyes closed, and we must admit that we were not well prepared for the crisis. However, we must also acknowledge that it would be unfair for the current government to impose responsibility on all the revealed structural problems arising from the period since the independence of the government, and the economic and financial crisis has destroyed them to the end and flooded them to the surface. While this is to some extent an excuse for the current situation but not entirely. Citizens who are only struggling to survive do not have an interest in excuses. The consequences of the crisis in Slovenia are also felt more strongly because economic growth was under-based on increasing productivity and improving competitiveness. This fact appears to be a key weakness in the Slovenian economy at the time of the crisis and is hampering faster recovery. In addition, we are faced with the fact that, in the past economic period, both the state and most of the companies that have been in difficulties today have been in excessive or unnecessary debt and that they must be due in difficult economic circumstances when the logic dictates otherwise. Therefore, the Government has now shown more determination to offer, in addition to the long-term set of reforms and changes, a set of operational rescue measures mainly to consolidate public finances, improve the conditions for the operation of businesses, improve payment discipline, improve the functioning of the labour market, improve the enforcement of economic crime and structural measures and institutional adjustments. We support the proposed measures in the parliamentary group, but we point out that two are needed at the critical point of consolidation of public finances, both deficits and public debt, which implies higher economic growth and structural reforms. The consolidation of public finances, both deficits and public debt requires a reduction of the cyclical and structural deficit. First, it implies higher and sustainable economic growth, secondly structural reforms. We caution that the fast-track reduction of the budget deficit is leading to the cancellation of development. Like most countries and communities, the European Union has responded to the crisis by means of measures that it has financed with a marked increase in the deficit. These measures were sufficient to halt the progress of the crisis, its spread into depression. Even before reliable signs of a more lasting recovery, the EU has developed the opposite policy, a policy of accelerated fiscal consolidation and debt reduction through sharp austerity measures. The pace of the projected deficit reduction implies the uncompromising application of different belt tightening policies. For the reasons mentioned above, it is obvious that there is a great and serious possibility that such measures will not be implemented, since, in the case of internal and external commitments adopted, the country will necessarily lead to additional indebtedness and, above all, to a dramatic reduction of the developmental parts of the budget, investment and all other forms of investment in development. In such circumstances, even the possible fiscal balance achieved does not mean otherwise than appearance, as the country will continue to increase the development backlog with all the unrelenting consequences. The framework currently in place in the EU, which is expected to be fully and in short Slovenia, is viewed by many economists as excessive and will prevent member governments from starting the economy due to reduced demand. Therefore, the restoration of growth and fiscal consolidation require a different approach; instead of the German-French wagon and the tightening of the belt, it would be better to use the funds for the actual start-up of the investment cycle. Secondly, we really need political solutions first. We find that no one wants political change and everyone just wants to survive. However, the price of this political blockade is high in economic and development terms, and political and economic costs are high. The issue of the priority of the coalition after half the term of office actually shows us as a web dilemma and as a whole the complexity of Slovenian politics and countries. The awareness of the seriousness of these blockades is too small. That is why we have already warned in the really long time ago about barriers to development. In the first place, we need to look at ourselves and find that we are faced with the fatal incompetence of the majority of the political class. The main reason for the inefficiency and growing lack of confidence in democracy is the unwillingness of the political class to focus its attention on the design and adoption of development decisions. When Slovenian policy talks about important reforms, it uses a tested formula, we are for change, but: if and until nothing changes. The problem is not so much in the agreement on the objectives themselves, but in the awareness and acceptance of the consequences of achieving the agreed priorities. In short, we need to remove political blockades first. For a policy which, due to the peace of the house, or the party's detrimentalness, or the flirting with public opinion, or the subjecting of capital interests, subject to local interests, does not take urgent decisions or monitor the failure to implement agreed policies and does not act, constitutes the abandonment of a fundamental responsibility in the management of public affairs, the protection of public interest and the long-term development of Slovenia.

The reason for this is an extremely important topic which concerns the future of the Slovenian state and its economy and thus the future of practically all citizens and citizens of the Republic of Slovenia. The Government's actions are those that will shape our destiny, so we need to find a broader consensus in Slovenian society, and we can also contribute greatly to the search for these solutions. These measures or priorities are certainly needed by the President of the Government; also because they concern social interventions, where DeSUS Members are warned or warmly recommended to the Government to reach the broadest possible social consensus, as it is only in this way that a measure can be effectively implemented. On the contrary, we can experience the so-called'retirement reform', which we do not know today which direction it will take and which has caused much discomfort and intolerance between citizens and citizens. Taking such a course without social consensus is certainly not good. As far as solving the problem of payment discipline is concerned, we have at this very session some laws that go towards disciplining payers. Whether these proposals are good or not, we have also had a lot of concerns and comments in our Group of Members and the answer to this question will in fact be given at this meeting, when we will also consider these proposals and, if they prove appropriate, also confirm them. Work talks have also been ongoing for some time on more effective prosecution of economic and organised crime. One solution is also the seizure and seizure of illegally acquired property. How this will be resolved from a procedural or implementing point of view, so that such a measure will be effective and implemented as soon as possible, will need to be addressed, in particular in terms of the burden of proof. However, I can point out right now that the DeSUS Group will pay particular attention to the functioning of the labour market and the modernisation of the health system. The planned changes are expected to go towards simpler, faster and more efficient procedures for concluding and terminating labour relations, facilitating transitions between jobs and employers. While such flexibility is welcome, it is only in certain circumstances — at a time of economic prosperity, when more jobs than applicants or searchers are readily available. Today, more than 115 thousand active job seekers are registered — yes, 115 thousand! Last night I watched a TV show Good idea, where a very good and important Slovenian economist also said that for less difficult jobs he does not get any workers. That's where it's supposed to be. In such circumstances, flexibility, as such, does not constitute any security, so we cannot exactly talk, as the Prime Minister says, about "safe flexibility". With regard to the issue of discharges and the shortening of notices, both issues have already been the subject of the amendment of the Labour Relations Act, which was rejected last year in the National Assembly, the position of the DeSUS Group has not changed. Changes towards reducing the rights of only and only older workers are by no means acceptable to us, despite the fact that the labour market regulation law has increased the time taken to receive unemployment compensation by one month, since the cancellation period would have been reduced by two months; and despite the fact that the labour market regulation law had imposed a slightly higher amount of compensation for the first three months, as it would have reduced by 40 % for a round of workers with 25 years and more working hours in the same employer. Since it was an intervention in the rights of older workers, who are the most vulnerable and vulnerable group at the time of employment, because they forgive this group the most quickly, and even more so this group of people is vulnerable than the category of unemployed, because older people do not like to work, we strongly opposed such changes in the DeSUS Group. From the point of view of DeSUS, the proposed regulation was too harsh to address the rights of workers who, in terms of their best years in terms of efficiency of work, left the company with their knowledge and loyalty to the employer, contributed significantly to the company's well-being and consequently increased capital. I can say that in the DeSUS Group in the future we will also strongly oppose the deprivation and reduction of the rights of older workers, especially because the possible introduction of the new pension law, which prolongs working activity, must create an environment that is friendly and safe for the category of older workers. As regards the modernisation of the healthcare system in the DeSUS Group, it is estimated that there is going to be a recast of the healthcare system with well-thought solutions in favour of patients. We believe that Minister Marušič is on the right track, in particular through the planned rationalisation of the health network, the abolition of supplementary health insurance, the more rigorous regulation of waiting species, where unfortunately we are still witnessing species skipping, and similar solutions. We are also looking forward to redefining the health care basket from compulsory health insurance, which we are also convinced will also go towards protecting patients' rights and maintaining the accessibility of health services to all patients, regardless of their social and economic status. In short, in the DeSUS Group we are in principle committed to the Government's actions, both in the short term and in the longer term. However, I repeat, not at the expense of reducing the rights of any category of persons, but at the same time the older, young, employed, unemployed. We will also actively cooperate with our own, sometimes perhaps slightly different views from that of the Government, and we hope and expect that our proposals will not run into closed doors like, for example, pension reform, but will be the basis for further constructive negotiations and coordination. It is more than welcome to hear about this subject not only the reply of the President of the Government to the question raised, but, honourable Members and Members, also your thoughts. That is why I and the other DeSUS Members are pleased that you supported our proposal for a debate expressed at the previous meeting. I am confident that today's debate will contribute to better solutions, proposals that will soon be back on our tables, and which, I believe, will ultimately contribute to a more stable economy in our country and, as a result, to greater social security for all people. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u14	Thank you very much for your word, Madam President. Greetings to the President of the Government, ministers and ministers, co-workers, colleagues and colleagues! Of course, there are times when politics must also talk about this, and I must say that with the answers of the Prime Minister, I am pleased. However, it is often necessary to realize that the hand that works, the head that thinks, certainly does more good than the mouth that criticizes, speaks, and any adjective could give it. The fact is that the economic and financial crisis, despite the gradual recovery, has caused major macroeconomic imbalances in the country, particularly within the euro area. At the time of economic recovery, increasing the competitiveness of enterprises in foreign markets, as well as the domestic business environment, and in particular making Slovenia different, attractive to investors, is essential to boost growth. During the period of economic crisis, the European Union's developmental catch-up has certainly been halted, which is largely linked to structural reasons, and this is what must be our priority and concern. The lower productivity is due to the structure of the Slovenian economy. It is important to be aware that we have a smaller share of technology-intensive and knowledge-based services and that we also have a lower level of productivity of individual sectors, where there is still considerable potential to increase the added value of employees, especially if we intensify research and innovation activities and improve human capital. These facts proved to be a key weakness of the Slovenian economy and an obstacle to faster heating during the economic crisis. There is also a lack of effective strengthening of knowledge society factors, which are reflected in weak and entrepreneurship-related research and development activities and the low efficiency of university education, among the reasons for non-competitiveness. This particular education is insufficiently aligned with the demand for graduates in the labour market, although some efforts have been made to reduce administrative barriers to the development of entrepreneurship. On the other hand, complicated bureaucratic procedures continue to make it difficult to create and operate businesses. On the other hand, on the other hand, relatively this labour market is hampered by the faster elimination of non-profitable parts of the economy. This is not only a limitation on faster entrepreneurial development, but also on the inflow of foreign direct investment, which can play an important role in raising the level of productivity of enterprises by transferring knowledge and technology. In addition, in the short term, the biggest obstacles to speeding up the economy are the lack of payment discipline and the unstable financial system or its inefficient operation in the area of providing the necessary funding for businesses. Slovenia has worsened the competitive situation in export markets in recent years by measuring it with cost competitiveness and market shares. We have also come to the forefront of the world's most important indicators of economic global competitiveness and business simplicity, as we have been overtaken by some of the new members of the European Union, who were far behind us at the time of the entry of the European Union. Therefore, the active role of the business sector in improving the competitiveness of the economy is crucial. Measures to improve competitiveness are an important step in the delivery of obstacles to faster development and the creation of a more conducive business environment, but they certainly cannot replace the efforts of the economy to improve their products and services. It is important to take an entrepreneurial initiative to effectively channel resources into research – more for innovation at entrepreneurial level – and it is also important to improve how to improve the quality of a large part of Slovenian management. Behind us is the round half of the leadership mandate we received at the elections at the end of 2008. At the time, people wanted change, they wanted to see a policy that acted on the principle of inflating economic growth of questionable quality to stop the policy of social exclusion, plundering and exhaustion of state-owned enterprises, controversial arms deals, media subjugation, politicisation of the prosecution, and the defamation of everyone and everyone through pre-election free. It is a fact, but it is not an excuse that we have taken over the leadership of the country in difficult times, since almost as soon as the day of the oath came through the wide open door until then a carefully hidden economic crisis also entered. We knew it wasn't going to be like it was. We knew that we would have to tighten our belts and deal with the crisis. During the two years of the crisis, Slovenia, compared with most European countries, needs to say this out loud and also admit it, has done much better, although critics, dissatisfactions and whistleblowers try to prove the exact opposite every day in this country. But once again I will say what I said yesterday: whoever doesn't trust himself, who humbles himself, is the basis and will be the singing of strangers, and competition and competitors will be able to take advantage of it. According to all objective indicators, such as debt rates, unemployment rates and poverty, we are certainly in the lower third, i.e. in a third of the countries in the European Union that have been successful in this area. The result of such a successful mitigation of social pressures is also that Slovenia has remained relatively calm and secure compared to other European countries, where dissatisfaction is not expressed in street violence. An important point, an important paragraph is also the pursuit of economic crime. This area has been presented by the Government as one of the most important priorities of its activities. This is an important message to citizens and citizens who rightly require such acts to be terminated, detected and duly sanctioned, and everything obtained from such criminal activity is also properly confiscated and returned to the State budget. Today, we see who has contributed to this growth – especially today's declining construction sector. In this area, however, the opposition today does not come up with any suggestions on how to cool down this overheating and stifle the stagnation of this sector. A number of measures and a number of actions could be more effective, but nevertheless I must stress once again that the path and action to respond to the crisis was, in the opinion of the SD Group. The government and the Group of the Social Democrats do not want Slovenia to be again in a situation such as that of the financial crisis and the great recession. This is why we are taking measures to prepare Slovenia for possible difficult situations in the future. This is a priority of 6 which is crucial to the work of the Government by the end of this term. With the consolidation of public finances, we will gradually try to reduce the public financial deficit and move closer to 3% of GDP by 2013, because, despite saving, we want to maintain the developmental orientation of the budget and promote economic growth and employment. On the other hand, it is true that the implementation of the consolidation of public finances depends in particular on the success of the pension reform. The Group of Social Democrats is aware of this that, in the absence of a pension reform or a consolidation of public finances, this would have an impact on the reduction of the country's credit rating, which would have deepened the crisis and worsened the prospects for recovery of the economy, which had been overly indebted in the good times of the merger, including through the actions of the previous governments. Failure to implement pension reform would result in more costly debt for new loans and thus higher interest rates for the country, municipalities, businesses and, last but not least, for us citizens, but also lower wages, higher unemployment, lower economic growth and consequently lower pensions. The adoption of pension reform is therefore necessary in our opinion if we are to have decent pensions, despite the fact that the number of employees is decreasing and the number of pensioners is increasing. Health-care measures are also important for achieving affordable and quality healthcare services with financial sustainability and rationalising the delivery of health services. In the SD Group, we also support the fact that the Government does not neglect the current problems faced by companies. This is why we support a package of measures aimed at improving payment discipline. Otherwise, the National Assembly adopted a solution on payment discipline at the beginning of this government's mandate. As of January 2010, we have reduced the VAT refund to 21 days, despite the fact that these were the requirements of the previous government, before the Pahor government, which would, of course, be much easier to carry out. We have also already introduced the country's payment deadlines for 30 days, of course we are now setting the maximum payment deadline for public authorities, which is no more than 30 days. With the proposal for a law on the prevention of late payments, we will regulate payment discipline by introducing a 60-day deadline for payment and the introduction of compulsory payments, which will most likely put the culture of avoidance of payments on the table. Solutions to the amendments to the value added tax law will provide for a prohibition on the enforcement of the exit VAT if the VAT liability does not pay the invoices within the prescribed time limit. The Government is also preparing changes in the field of the law on enforcement and insurance, which is intended to ensure greater effectiveness of enforcement itself. In the parliamentary group, we also support amendments to the Financial Business Act, insolvency and forced termination procedures, which will allow greater protection of creditors of the insolvency debtor. It will therefore be necessary to supplement the enforcement procedures by requiring the minimum share of creditors' payments to be prescribed and the maximum period of time to pay these claims. The government has also set a special priority to improve the conditions for business operations. The objective of this priority is to enable companies to be restructured as quickly as possible, to provide businesses with as transparent and easy access to the various financial instruments offered by the State. As a result, on 1 July 2010, the Government adopted the concept of a development platform, which will run through the Bank's SID as a national development and incentive institution, which can become an important instrument of the new Slovenian development model. As a result, the development platform will be one of the key measures that will help to restructure the economy in increasing the added value in addition to the measures already implemented by the Government or implemented in the economic field. It should also be borne in mind that the State, through the SID of the Bank, financed 2.8 billion projects during the crisis, which contributed significantly to the developmental financing of businesses during the crisis. In fact, the Government is changing the wrong development concept of the former government when subsidies still served as a mechanism for the survival of old, large, large and large public enterprises, and not as a mechanism for promoting the development of promising enterprises, which is also the direction of Slovenia's development strategy. The development innovation strategy also seeks to follow the aim of establishing a modern research and innovation system that will allow for better quality of life for all, using the critical reflection of society, to effectively address societal challenges. On the basis of the priority of improving the functioning of the market, it is particularly important to improve employment, which is an acute problem in Slovenia. The government has already adopted a key law in this area in 2010, the Labour Market Regulation Act. This year, the law began to be implemented and its main objective is to increase the safety of job seekers in a certain part of the introduction of the concept of safe flexibility in the labour market. We think that the new law will increase efficiency in the implementation of active employment policy measures, which is important for enhancing the social security of vulnerable groups in the labour market. We have also adopted the Small Work Act, which will be able to connect key groups of people with the labour market; these are unemployed students and pensioners. The law is not yet in force, but it is important in terms of maintaining contact with the labour market, especially the unemployed and pensioners who want to work. With the recast of labour law, we will further implement the concept of safe flexibility in the labour market. In the area of justice, the Government has set a priority for targeting measures that will help to prosecute economic crime more effectively. Economic crime has probably been present in Slovenia for a long time, not just in recent years, perhaps since 1991. During what period, there may have been more of him, and in some other period, he has been persecuted for the past two years. ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u24	Thank you very much. Dear Members and Members! On what has already been said, I would not like to be disputed today because the documents exist, there are also statements from the past to this day, so that we will not do much with words. Perhaps it is more simply to find out whether the facts are the same as we see them, because there is probably no need for us to unify ourselves because we are going to be difficult, but we should probably do so about the facts. First of all, what's going on in Europe since 2005, say, until 2011. Europe experienced from 2005 to 2008, a growth that was not normal for the whole European economy – not before – at least 15 years before. And this rise did not arise in the maximum "Europe is the most competitive area in the world" but in the relatively poorly managed fiscal policy and in the development of those activities that have shown the greatest difficulties in the crisis. Slovenia was not the only one caught up in construction or construction activities. Some countries today in the worst crisis had similar concepts of development. Spain and, for example, Portugal and partly Ireland are such countries that, when they were looking for where to invest the free financial resources generated by the created social product, decided on internal development, it is said, not competitive development, but non-competitive development. Therefore, promotion of construction activities, real estate transactions based on imports of cheap labour and on relatively low wages for this labour force. Here Spain is a typical example, and Ireland in a similar way. On the other hand, we had countries like Greece that spent far beyond their borders even in the best of times, and the story simply ended very simply by the fact that the country fell not into the liquidity crisis, but into the crisis of insolvency. This has not yet been definitively recognised by Europe as it is making decisions gradually, as aspirin is taken for pain, although pain is a sign of some serious problem. Only a few countries in Europe could benefit from the results today from the period 2005-2010 and 2008, better said. One of them was Germany, which went through an extremely difficult period of adjustment if we leave the period immediately after the German merger, but mainly in the early 2000s, when, although it is a social state, even after the constitution, it tried to catch the late train of competitiveness from before this period – and it also caught it. The countries that did not catch this are, in particular, countries of the Mediterranean pool such as Italy, such as Spain and some others, Greece, of course, and some others. Slovenia's found itself here somewhere in the middle. What has been said today is that before 2008, things in the competitiveness field were going great – unfortunately they were not going well. We were driven by the European cycle of growth, cyclical growth, but there were many problems that had already been accumulated. After all, the structural deficit also in Slovenia, this structural deficit in public finances shows what is happening without the supplement created by the short-term recovery of economic growth over the normal growth that is sustainable over the long term. Our structural deficit has not improved in these years. So we took advantage of the economic cycle, which, as we say, was above average good throughout Europe, for us to swim on it, but we failed to do it inside, although the previous government had at the outset some clear definitions of economic reform, which were largely not achieved; it is said to be good objectives, good vision, and, procedurally, relatively weak implementation. What we now see is, let's say, in this period of time, as I would say, economically speaking, of course, much more difficult. If we look at this thing, our competitiveness has deteriorated since 2007, quietly, but like the high pressure of a man who did not feel excessive, there has been little dizziness, but there has been no more. Even if we look at public finances, in 2008, when the year was still normal with 3% growth, we ended up with a government deficit of almost 2%. If we look at the structural deficit, it was so much bigger. So there was a day when what would have come to Slovenia in any case, even if there had not been such a severe crisis as the one that hit us, not only us, but also the others. As far as employment is concerned, we have significantly increased employment not in highly skilled jobs, but mostly in low-skilled jobs, because if you look at what jobs have been lost in the post-discharge period, jobs that are mainly low-paid or in low-value industries or in low-value-added activities have been lost. Construction is a high responsibility for the implementation of works on the motorway network, which is good physically because we have built an additional infrastructure system, and economically, it has not contributed much to this economic growth to structurally capture. Nor would I have been caught because the end of this cycle would have ended sooner than before without a crisis. We couldn't build a highway indefinitely. This project at the bottom of the Sava is a commendable project, but it is not a national project in terms of added value in the economy, it is a physically very important project, economically speaking for employment, but what has been said does not have any very dramatic impact on the increase in the level of employment in the economy, because it is largely capitalised and is therefore very small. This also applies to the end of motorways, because at the time we built motorways, we employed a lot of people at the quality of jobs. What bothers Mr Viszak – having mentioned as his first priority employment – is employment, which can indeed happen, not through public investment, but through autonomous private investment. Where companies invest themselves, not because the state encourages them to do so, but because they have a business interest in modernising, accelerating, expanding their activities, not only domestically, especially in foreign markets. In this case, too, a relief that would bring 100 % of the investment relief would not mean much, since at this point the problem is not an easy one for investments, but a problem of overindebtedness. And this is not solved by facilitating profit, but by restructuring investments and reducing the burdens that are financial in nature from the repayment of commitments. And then, in the end, I'm going to say something about the measures on this subject. So it is not that employment in us is not a central element of action, because, after all, employment is not a goal for itself, but an instrument for the well-being of citizens and citizens, it is said that they can live materially better than they could have done without it. Thus, employment is not the goal, but it is the means to raise personal and common standards in the country, and the measures that we are discussing, which we may or may not have positions more or less similar, are aimed at, because if economic growth in Slovenia is not without additional employment, we have a relatively low presence of additional capital coming from abroad because it is relatively small in Slovenia. So, the combination of capital and work in this case is aimed at ensuring that as many people in Slovenia are actively active employees but not employees in order to be able to benefit from social benefits, but are employed in order to receive from this job such a salary as is provided by relatively high added value. If we look at this moment, which economic activities are lagging behind – mainly economic activities with high added value, with the exception of one exception, this is the financial sector, which may be somewhat further behind – trade, construction and agriculture are lagging behind. So these are activities, if you look at the added value that was published by the statistical office in the publication of this 2010 economic growth data, then very nicely began to concentrate weaknesses around sectors that we knew were poorly competitive and low-productive. Many problems in agriculture are not only due to trade but also due to the relatively poor manufacturing industry and the primary sector. There have been many investments, private and others in agriculture, which unfortunately do not produce those results even in primary production that might be expected from these investments. The same is true of course in construction, where everything is not bad. It is bad that the companies are prisoners, these construction companies are captives of those owners who cannot work with these companies; and in this case, the justice system is the one that has to break the owner's relationship to the company as soon as possible, that is to say, the owners that have led to the non-solvent situation. For example, a few cases of larger companies show that this process takes a very long time and is taking place too slowly. At this point, there are some construction companies that could peacefully continue to operate in the future, because they have good technology and also have a lot of engineers, and so on, if they were burdened by this relationship that is now being handled by the owners of these companies. Labour costs in Slovenia increased significantly in 2009 more than on average in the European Union due to the implementation of the Law on Public Sector Salaries. It's an empirical fact, you don't have to argue about it. If you look at the data, then you get a big boost in wages, which is a common wage in Slovenia, not only in the public sector, you run above a level that would be sustainable in terms of the growth of the social product. These are the industries that first rode a tiger that ran heavily since 2007 and then couldn't jump out of that tiger when the tiger got tired. Construction and partly trade – here are the basic cores of non-payment. And this isn't about managers not wanting to pay. Here is the basic way of not paying the bill, including the signed contract. After all, construction companies have blown up this payment indiscipline throughout the country through their deliberate actions — non-payment! It's not true that we're out of money! Listen, if in this case there was such a construction conjuncture between 2005 and 2007, where did this money go in 2008, 2009 and 2010 that it was gone? And that you can no longer pay for things that have been paid by, say, the countries when it came to public projects – where did this money go? Therefore, it is not true that these companies did not have the economic power to implement their contracts. This has always been a way of doing business in the construction sector. You know this also for those of you who have been more actively involved in this sector in recent years. This has been going on ever since Slovenia's independence. I can guarantee this because I was, unfortunately, involved in this even when we had to argue with some companies, whether they would pay their subcontractors – in 1998 and today we are in 2011. So it's not about finding me a solution now that's economically logical and acting on its own. In this case, an administrative act must force such undertakings to pay, but if they do not want to pay, they cannot benefit from a deduction of value added tax. Why not? For a simple reason, because the person who performed the service paid the tax in full. The one who received this service and could only account for it, provided that he has fulfilled his contractual obligations, since this is the basic principle of the functioning of VAT – we are not talking about paid realization, we are talking about contractual obligations – and since the contractual obligations do not pay, he has no right to deduct something he has not paid. This is a very simple fact and this cannot be resolved by some elegant measure, but can only be resolved by setting a certain period within which accounts must be paid. We have even given the possibility that, for excusable reasons, the so-called "clients" can also agree on this, but after this period, the person who did not do this has a choice: whether he will pay and refuse the tax, but if he does not pay, this will have to go into multilateral compensation temporarily. This is not all-powerful, but some of these mutual obligations will, nevertheless, to a greater extent be settled. There is no better measure in the short term for such things, unless the state of tops pours into the system, say, two billion euros. But I'd throw them in the wrong place, like in construction, into some companies that are insolvent, not insolvent, they're insolvent. Why they are insolvent, this can be discussed. And such a company is best at the shortest possible time to separate from ownership, because the company still has equipment, good employees, good engineers and so on, it could still get a business, as it is now, and it can no longer get it. That's why these measures were. The second measure was, which is in Parliament, regarding the Public Procurement Act. I hope you will support the idea that within this, if those who wish to obtain this order cannot prove that they have settled obligations, for the first time, and for the second time, if they are willing to disclose their ownership relationships at home and abroad, they can apply for a public contract if they do not want to, but cannot apply. It's his choice, and here we stop, we don't play anything. Those who do not wish to disclose this have this right and will then not participate in a public invitation to tender or in public procurement. It's very simple. Otherwise, this problem will not be solved; we talk about much, quiet society and so on. What the state can do is to solve this problem in the relationship itself as an economic operator with the private sector in this case – those who want to do these works. Another time. It is quite clear that from now on, payments will run directly to subcontractors. There is no other option for the State, which means that this information on subcontractors must be clearly expressed, if not, then those competing in public procurement cannot compete in this procurement. In this way, the state can manage what is still possible. However, if the general principle is that contracts which are signed are not respected, the other solution will not be, as if the state can regulate what it can manage; in private relations between private entities, if they do not wish to comply with the contracts, the only possibility is for this to be settled in a court of law. There's no third option. Although there is no reason for this discipline not to be restored in Slovenia, because I think that in the main part of other sectors, in industry and elsewhere, there is no such non-discipline as in the case of construction and partly because of the market situation in the area of trade. And why is it that the Chamber of Commerce is protesting the most right now? Because the longer payment periods have been the most disrupted since the relationship between those participating in this market is uneven. This is true, the market situation is unbalanced. Why did this happen? Because trade was able to concentrate in 15 years, but the manufacturing industry was not, it was heavily fragmented and unrelated. And in this way, there are these situations that you now know on the market. In this case, it can react to the Office for the Protection of Competition and has the right to look into contracts between business partners and can see such types of play happening or conditions occurring in trade. Do you think that large retail chains in Slovenia pay their suppliers outside Slovenia within 180 days? Of course not. The next thing that was said today is liquidity. Three proposals are contained in these documents, which very clearly state three things. First time. If we want companies to be able to function better on average again, given the structure of their balance sheets and their costs, it is essential to apply the principle of additional investment in businesses. If the banks are unable to do so due to undercapitalisation, there is a sufficient amount of free capital in Europe, which is also willing to enter the Slovenian economy and co-invest in the renewal of the balance sheet of many Slovenian companies. Those who don't want to do this won't cooperate, those who want to do this would have a chance. As far as banks are concerned, I am simply warning: do not be naive and think that a bad bank as a state institution will solve the issue of sub-capitalisation of banks in Slovenia. Not for at least two reasons. For the first time, because this concept does not know in what way and what claims to buy from banks, and we will be in a situation where the state is too expensive to buy from the public and, in the view of the banks, too cheap. This problem must be resolved within banks; if necessary, also through additional recapitalisation and not only by countries but also by other entities. That's the first time. Another time. By rationalizing your own business. In our case, the balance sheet total of employees in banks is lower than the European average. So there are many elements to rationalising intra-bank business. And the third time. Why are we proposing a bill on the taxation of the balance sheet sum of banks? For a very simple reason. Not to gain additional fiscal resources, as some other European countries do, but to encourage greater emphasis within the structure of their balance sheets on loans to the non-financial sector, it is said to economic companies and businesses to individuals. The tax would be made in such a way that banks would have to pay a balance sheet tax, but they would receive tax relief on the extent of credit, the state of credit in the economy or businesses. Therefore, those who increase their credits more than the total balance sheet sum or lower their credits than the balance sheet total will not be taxed. Those who do not do so will be taxed. This is therefore an incentive measure in order to make bank behaviour policies more positive for the normal renewal of credit activity, normally according to the economic cycle, within the limits that exist and set by regulators. As far as the financing of businesses, which I think is important to us, so that we can get new jobs and that these jobs would be where we need them most, and this is where we can also achieve the highest added value, is the way to finance businesses. Every year we have about a billion euros to finance small, medium and large enterprises, in combination with the SID Bank, the Slovenian Enterprise Fund, the Regional Fund, not to mention the Housing Fund. The problem that exists is that they are not well connected, business policies are largely uncoordinated so far, and that these institutions have not specialized in the area that they most manage: the Enterprise Fund for Investments in Initial Enterprises or in Seed Enterprises, in the so-called risk capital, and the SID Bank for projects that are larger in the Slovenian space, including, say, for the construction of hydropower plants in the lower Sava, something else is probably also important. In combination with resources existing in the European Union, the EIB and other institutions. The SID Bank is the most appropriate institution to attract these funds to Slovenia and thus enable the smaller financial institutions that I have mentioned to be able to perform their function in a qualitative manner. As I said, a billion euros a year is not a small amount, the question is whether or not it can be used qualitatively. But I'm done with public finances because I didn't want to start with them. In public finances, however, there will be a fundamental issue in the coming years, not only for this government, but also for the next governments, or we are able to do so, because by 2013, the deficit will have to be reduced to below 3%, and then within at least two to three years, to an approximate level playing field. This will not be possible if it is within the structure of public finances – and here I am not only referring to the state budget, but also to municipalities that have not suffered any major distortions so far, as far as funding is concerned in times of crisis, if you look at the figures and rates of expenditure growth in this area – that there will be a transfer of expenditure disproportionately to the area of promoting competitiveness, research and implementation of this in the business sector and infrastructure. These are three important priority areas in public finances. At the same time, it is necessary to retain in some way what has so far been intended for social transfers, especially for the pension system and the health allowance. In doing so, additional short-term employment in the public sector should be stopped. And this law will now come to the government in a short time because it has not been possible, with any negotiation, nor with social partners, to achieve any movement in this area, not in the past years, not in the last two years. If we look at how public sector employment has grown, we can see that the fastest growth has taken place in education, health and social care. In these two, three sectors have been growing quite well since 2000, or calmly every year, regardless of the circumstances. When we have to deal with this in substance, we would touch on the norms and standards in these areas, which are not necessarily 100 per cent used in any situation, but we can approach this with some reasonable process of improving the standard. In the short term, of course, we can also find some reserves in this area that will help us achieve the primary priorities at the same time and not neglect these areas in a way that would require forgiveness or something like that. Therefore, we will propose to stop additional employment in these segments for the next two years, so that natural fluctuations are possible. Secondly, the problem of public finances will be that if we do not reduce the public financial deficit, the interest on debt financing and debt repayment will exceed that level which would allow for the normal distribution of priorities within public financial expenditure. Last but not least, it will be difficult and difficult for the future to put into place within these programmes, which we now have in the state budget, but not in the municipalities, that the efficiency criteria are being applied: what happened in the programme, the sub-programme or the action on the euro money invested in the year, two, three of the effects envisaged in such a programme. And if they are not used, they are not realized, it is possible that such a programme will no longer exist in the state budget in the coming years. Only in this way will it be possible to meet these constraints, which are of a general nature, and at the same time to achieve that in the budget we can finance what will bring additional jobs, as has been said, while at the same time bringing those additional places that will have long-term economic, social and social benefits. I therefore believe that these measures and analyses, which have also been prepared in terms of competitiveness, are correct. We'll see about the performance. What has been said in the end by the President of the Government and this project, which is running in Europe, is obviously two things going on, and Slovenia will support what it means if we want to participate in projects such as competitiveness, we will also require a significantly more equitable burden-sharing when it comes to solving the financial crisis in Europe. A small country with relatively small financial systems cannot suffer the same financial burden as countries with large financial systems involved in a wide range of systems that are in countries that are currently in difficulty not to mention individual cases. As a result, we also demand certain things, and we also make certain things conditional on the adoption of these measures as a member of the Euro Group, because we believe that these measures at European level must be economically sound and especially fair in their design and implementation. ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u42	Thank you very much. Mr Vice President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! As mentioned several times, Mr Rezman also mentioned the issue of the labour market, I would like to address two issues which you have raised in the debate so far. The first question, which concerns all of us in some way, is the rise in unemployment, which we believe will be resolved, as the delay has begun to exacerbate, even with the delay. We estimate that over the next few months we should reach the peak of the number of unemployed and that they could gradually reduce this number. Nevertheless, let me add that, despite the 115 000 registered unemployed, there is a 7.8 surveyed unemployment in us, around 9.6 in the European Union, and we are still in that quarter of the countries where this rate is the lowest. It is particularly important that employment flows change, that we have already employed 57 000 people unemployed in 2010, which means 18% more than the year before when the crisis started to show sharp teeth, and that there were also 8.2%) more job vacancies, which is a good sign of expectations for this phenomenon in the future. In doing so, I must stress in particular that people are employed and are also losing their jobs, that this is a dynamic that is significant, and this is also a good sign that it is not only a loss of work, but also a new job. But since the question is about the reasons, I still have to say that there are reasons why the situation is as it is, quite a lot. I would point out in particular that the anti-crisis measures have expired, and this is one of the reasons why we have now received some additional unemployed persons. Nevertheless, I would like to stress what we have repeatedly stressed that 25 000 jobs have certainly been kept, and this has also been maintained permanently. If you look at the automotive industry, the electricity industry, these measures have gained a lot in this sense, that it can be very fast after the crisis has passed, responded to new orders that have started to occur, and many factories today, so to speak, have orders secured and even cannot accept everything. Of course, such a measure could not have had the same effect in unsuspecting industries. Another important reason is to raise the minimum wage, which we have taken consciously and deliberately, by around 24%, in order to protect the social situation of the lowest income categories, but also with the awareness that this will affect the restructuring of the economy, that therefore, under pressure of competition, jobs will probably disappear slowly, and in fact, as we note today, they will disappear to the greatest extent as a result. This, of course, leads to a tightening of the employment issue. In addition, it is important to stress that we can actually record when the number of unwanted jobs is decreasing, which causes, as it were, unemployment, to be seen also through the structure where the most unskilled workers are, while at the same time maintaining and regenerating more demanding jobs. Some of this has also contributed, we do not deny, to the pension reform, where legislation allows early retirement for some, even by applying as unemployed persons to avoid the effects that are expected when a new pension law is introduced. In particular, the recent construction crisis, which has been well analysed by Minister Gaspari, needs to be highlighted. I wouldn't go through with it anymore. For this analysis, I think it was well represented. But all I want to say is that when it did and the financial and construction balloon, of course, it turned out that there was a lot of growth from the previous period for, say, apparent growth that is now manifesting itself in all these problems, including in employment, and that we now have to solve these problems not only for domestic workers, but also for foreign workers, which this conjuncture has attracted mainly to undemanding jobs. The debate also raised the issue of job creation, which was particularly highlighted by Mr Vizjak. It is that, directly by the state, there is no indication of action here because there are only two options: whether we will be in further debt, which is constantly being debated in this assembly, or whether we will have to sacrifice social programmes on this account. Thus, direct job creation by the State is possible to a very limited extent. However, it is about creating indirect conditions for the economy to be able to revive and thus to get to such new jobs as we all want. To this end, much has been done by the various departments in terms of investing in development programmes by the Ministry of Economy and the Ministry of Higher Education, Science and Technology. If I mention only what is true in our department, you have a unique opportunity to support the Small Work Act at the referendum. It is expected to bring at least 10,000 jobs in the first step at the expense of the measures envisaged by this law. Today we will already discuss the law on prevention of employment and work in black – you have a unique opportunity to support this law, which is intended to facilitate or enable less work to be done in black, more self-employment, more employment. When it comes to measures in the field of employment policy, two days ago I explained that we will soon replace the collapse of the public works programme with other programmes for the same groups of people and for the same beneficiaries. The second issue to be addressed is the issue of working relations or the labour relationship law and what I call safe flexibility. I must say that in the last two years we have also invested primarily in increasing security in order to mitigate the effects of the crisis. This is about the intervention measures already mentioned, raising the minimum wage, regulating the labour market, social legislation, not least pension legislation. All this legislation goes in the direction of measures and instruments that increase human security. Of course, we must be aware that security without being able to finance it is not available and, of course, we need a strong economy that will provide adequate resources so that we can increase security. This economy also wants and needs to be able to respond more flexibly to supply and demand, including through a more flexible labour market. What has happened to us – not in the last year, but in the last decade – is that our labour market has actually fallen into two segments. One that is best illustrated by the part where full-time employment is concerned, for jobs occupied by older people in particular, partly by the middle generation, where jobs are extremely highly protected and where many of these are, we can say, also by virtue of this privilege. Long notices are noticeable, discharges are high, leave is long, allowances are paid for internships, these jobs are protected with regard to re-employment, education and the like. This goes so far as to have a paradoxical phenomenon today, when one of the older workers loses his job, it is actually very difficult to hire him back, because for these reasons it is simply for employers to avoid it. And then employers are looking for other options, other alternatives, employers looking for job flexibility. That is why we are expanding our work for a certain period of time, contractual work, student work and so on. There, in particular, we find young people working with minimum or no social rights. This, of course, creates not only a problem in the labour market but also intergenerational differences, which can and in fact also reduce intergenerational solidarity and create tensions at this level. It is precisely for these reasons that we must also cut into the field of labour legislation, the law on labour relations, and it is not about simply being able to abolish flexible forms of work, as we want to protect, through the Small Work Act, that the people who carry out these works will have a few more rights, and at the same time it will probably be necessary to increase flexibility on the part of the part where it is an indefinite period of time, and it will probably be necessary to cancel anything at this point. Let me say when it has already been mentioned that it is often mentioned in this regard in the OECD study, which has clearly shown that those countries with very high-security employment also have the most fixed-term employment, the most flexible forms of employment. Simply because employers are looking for employment opportunities where flexibility is greater. Spain is such a case and Slovenia is also one. And this is a clear signal that all forms of employment need to be more balanced, but we cannot do this only by making today more flexible forms less flexible, but also by making the least flexible slightly more flexible. And this is a difficult work that awaits us all, and I hope that we will find a common language also in these solutions, which will by no means be easy. Thank you very much.

In fact, with the work of the four specialised departments, in a way that will certainly enable the rapid operation of these departments, because their formation will no longer be solely linked to the interest or consent of interested judges. From now on, the Presidents of the courts will be the ones who can be assigned to this section. However, this includes the specialization of the prosecution. As it is known, we are preparing at the Ministry of Justice and will present to the Government this month a new law on the State Prosecutor's Office for decision, and this is a new specialised state prosecutor for the prosecution of more difficult forms of economic and organised crime. The main added value of this specialised prosecution group compared to today's group of state prosecutors is that the human resources will be significantly stronger, of course, with the very precise powers that this department will have. It is also important that specialised departments of the courts handle those cases that will be dealt with by the specialised prosecution. What are these matters, and they will be defined in the State Prosecutor's Office Act. It is not, therefore, that this package of so-called anti-Tacuna legislation would interfere with the permanent mandate of judges, as one of the Members pointed out. This is not even possible because a permanent mandate is a constitutional category and cannot be affected by the amendment of laws, but it is true that these two judicial laws will greatly strengthen the control of judges, even in specific open, non-legislative cases, and this control will always be exercised by the Presidents of the higher courts, so that neither policy nor anyone else can be involved in the trial process. Finally, let me add that this list of these laws, as far as they relate to the area of justice and which you have in this plan of the Government, is not, in fact, exhaustive because it is complemented by the amendment of the law on enforcement and the amendment of the insolvency law. They are both ready and will be submitted to the Government for decision no later than 14 days. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u96	Mr Vice-President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! You have before you the proposal for a law on prevention of work and employment in black. In the preparation of the new law, we have set ourselves, in particular, to increase the effectiveness of the supervisory authorities in detecting infringements in order to record clearer definitions and determine the greater powers of the supervisory authorities. The aim of the law is also to strengthen sanctions and to increase mushrooms in order to act educationally and in particular preventively. All the proposals and initiatives for this law have also been addressed by the Commission to prevent and detect work and employment in black. The proposal before you today is therefore based on intensive inter-sectoral coordination, cooperation with the social partners who agreed with the proposal and on concrete experience from the ground. One of the most important novelties of the law is that the Customs Administration is involved in the control of employment in black, allowing the gradual engagement of an additional 170 field inspectors. Customs inspectors already have a number of experience in various control functions, have mobile departments capable of detecting violations on the ground 24 hours a day, have appropriate equipment and powers. As a result of the inclusion of the customs administration as a control tool, the labour inspectorate, which will focus more intensively on the supervision of labour inspections, will be unloaded. In public, it has been the most receptive to the restriction of neighbourhood aid, despite the fact that such a restriction has already been covered by the current law and has been in force since 2000. The proposal for a new law does not limit neighbourly aid more than it has been limited by the law so far, but even more precisely defines it. Where work is carried out on immovable property or movable property linked to the activity of a legal person or self-employed person, it shall not restrict neighbouring aid between persons who do not have a registered activity. By no means does the bill intend to restrict neighbourly relations, but it regulates the situation and more effective supervision in the field of work and employment in black. In view of the fact that the regulation and definition of neighbourhood aid has not had any negative effects on Slovenian society or human relations, we are confident that even with the minimum complemented definition of neighbourhood aid they will not deteriorate. Neighbouring aid is allowed to continue under conditions and scope so as not to constitute unfair competition for entrepreneurs and will not serve as a disguise of actual work or employment on the line. The fight against work and employment in the dark for the bright future is the slogan of the public campaign launched in 2010 and has also been joined as patrons or supporters by the social partners. Of course, part of this is the preparation of a new law. The fact is that the area of prevention of work and employment in a number of areas and the overall problem of this area cannot be addressed only within the framework of this law, but also within the framework of sectoral legislation there is a need to provide a solution. To this end, a special government group led by the Ministry of Economy has been established. The task of that group is to review all existing legislation and formulate proposals for necessary changes in the field of prevention of the grey economy and place them systematically in sectoral legislation. The law on prevention of work and employment in black, which is before you today, represents an important step in the fight against the grey economy in Slovenia or against black work. It will contribute to new jobs, greater opportunities for entrepreneurship, strengthening social registers and greater fairness in the allocation of social transfers. We therefore propose that you confirm it by Members and Members of Parliament. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-03-02-SDZ5-Redna-25.ana.u98	Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. Mr. Minister with colleagues, colleagues and colleagues! The Committee on Labour, Family, Social Affairs and Disabled Persons is at its 27th session. 2. 2011 and on the first day following that of the same day as the parent working body, the Government submitted the proposal for a law on prevention of work and employment in black on the second reading to the National Assembly for consideration and adoption following a regular procedure. The members and members of the Committee received the following materials: opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service, opinion of the Council of State, opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service, comments of the public concerned and decisions of the Committee on Agriculture, Forestry and Food. In accordance with Rule 129 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, amendments to the draft law have been tabled by the SD, DeSUS, LDS, the Zares and LDS Group, the Polish SLS Group and the SDS Group. The Committee took note of the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service, which examined the draft law in terms of compliance with the Constitution and the legal system and the legislative and technical aspects. The legislative and legal service also made specific comments to the draft law on Article 30. The Committee was also given an opinion by the State Council, which supports the proposal with reservations. He made two specific comments to the text of the bill, namely paragraphs 11 and 15. At the meeting of the Committee, the opinion of the Council of State was presented in more detail by the Council of State, Mr Aloise Kovšca. In the introduction to the articles, the representative of the proposer, the Minister for Labour, Family and Social Affairs and the disabled presented briefly the fundamental reasons for the adoption of the bill and in particular its main solution. The key objectives of the bill are to increase the powers of supervisory authorities, to provide a basis for more efficient work by supervisory authorities, to define a clearer definition of individual concepts and measures of law and to strengthen sanctions for lawbreakers. The law partially transposes the legal order of the Republic of Slovenia, the Directive on Minimum Standards for Sanctions and Measures against Employers, illegally residing third-country nationals. The proposed amendments also aim at improving the existing situation, eliminating legal uncertainties and abuses. The proposal sets out a new sanction for employers who employ an unemployed or inactive person in black, restricts neighbourhood aid which will be prohibited in cases where it is partly linked to the activity of a legal person, i.e. where the work is related to the activity of a legal person or a self-employed person, and in cases where work is carried out on immovable property and movable property used to carry out an activity. The representative of the Legal and Legislative Service presented a written opinion and pointed out that the intention of the proposer is to limit or prevent work and employment in black to face certain constitutional rights or principles. They are specifically reminded by their services in the individual articles of the bill, and in the introductory part of the opinion, it specifically highlights Article 35 of the Constitution, which also gives rise to the general freedom of human conduct. In the context of general comments, it also draws attention to the provisions relating to control that would have been added to them, since, in the present form, there may be overlaps between the supervisory authorities. It also points to the fact that the draft law refers to certain laws that have not yet been enacted or will only be subject to legislative procedure, such as the Small Work Act and the Voluntary Act. In the debate on the individual articles and the amendments tabled, the members and members of the Committee and the invited particular attention were paid to the proposed solutions to the draft law relating to general provisions, concepts, activities other than black work or employment, neighbourhood assistance, work in their own directing, humanitarian and charitable work, short-term work, supervisory authorities and criminal provisions. With regard to the provision of Article 1, which defines what the Law on Prevention of Work and Black Employment stipulates, the view was expressed that the proposal for a law was based solely on the method of punishment. The main objective should be to reduce work to black rather than sanctioning. In particular, the provisions of Articles 11, 12 and 15 have also been exposed. With regard to criminal provisions, the view was also expressed that the penalties were significantly too high, even for natural persons. Some debaters, however, argued that it was right to radically increase penalties, thus making it easier to achieve the purpose of the law. In accordance with the second paragraph of Article 130 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee considered the amendments tabled and adopted the majority of the amendments of the SD, DeSUS and LDS groups and the Zares and LDS groups on 12 and 15. However, he did not accept the other amendments or make certain amendments irrelevant. The Committee has also drawn up its two amendments to Articles 18 and 27. In accordance with Rule 128 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the Committee voted and adopted all the articles of the bill together. In the light of the amendments adopted and pursuant to the first paragraph of Article 133 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, the text of the amended law, which includes the amendments adopted, is prepared. This amended proposal for a law is also under consideration today. Thank you very much.

Exceptions include self-direction work, work in humanitarian and natural disasters, humanitarian and voluntary work, personal complementary work and short-term work as assistance, for example, within family companies. The law also imposes higher fines, as it is a reinforced sanctioning of the promise to take a firmer stand on this social issue. In doing so, it is important to stress that these changes are not isolated but part of the broader related measures. One of these is also a small job that offers different people, young people, the unemployed and pensioners, a legal, secure alternative to black work, which preserves flexibility and which, with the effectiveness of the fight against employment and black work, will significantly increase the scope compared to current student work. For all these reasons, the Social Democrats will support a new bill against employment and black work.

However, we are not going to agree that neighbourhood aid is not fully considered as an exception to black work. So far, the definition of neighbourhood aid has been inadequate and the proposal of this law has only made it worse. Therefore, we suggest that neighbourhood aid is not fully considered as black work unless someone benefits from this material benefit. Identifying neighbouring aid as work in black will not contribute in any way to increasing tax contributions, as it is clear that it is very difficult in practice to implement and, on the other hand, it causes unnecessary harassment of people and damage to neighbourly relations. We have taken a constructive approach to the bill, have tabled substantial amendments, so we expect the coalition to adopt our amendments and, above all, the public's expectations of neighbouring aid.
